### 1.13 What a session must not file, and what a filing must not touch (RS)

*Added 2026-08-09 (sessions), from `F-35`. Hermes Agent ships an exclusion list naming five things a
session produces that look like knowledge, and reading its source is what put them here. The list is
the reusable half of that project: its cadence does not transfer (`F-31`: no session-end ceremony,
two nudge counters at ten), its cost does not transfer (`F-32`: a forked reviewer at ~30,000 tokens
an event), and its prior inverts (`F-34`, decision BB). The failures the list names transfer whole,
because each one is a property of what a working session leaves behind rather than of how Hermes
reviews it.*

*Two more rules sit in the same table and come from the same source. `RS-141` and `RS-142` are the
guards Hermes enforces in code rather than in its review prompt (`F-36`), and this design needs both:
one refuses an amendment to bytes nobody loaded this turn, and one records a write's origin in the
file so nothing autonomous edits what the human wrote. Decision BC carries them.*

**RS-135 to RS-139 are enforced by a prompt, and they are the only rules in this document that are.**
Every other rule makes its guarantee structural, because RS-17's standard is that a signature or a
permission beats a sentence. Nothing structural can decide whether a proposed sentence is a working
method or a record of five things that failed, since both are prose about the same session and
neither is checkable from bytes. So the rules below are honest about their mechanism instead: the
list is one module constant, `LEARN_EXCLUSIONS` in `research.py`, rendered verbatim into the
distillation context that RS-102 assembles, never re-worded per call and never split across prompts.
The acceptance tests are two, and both are stated per rule: a **no key** assertion that the constant
reaches the context byte for byte, and a **live** assertion over a golden session record built to
produce exactly that defect. `RS-140` is what keeps the whole set from being read as advice, and it
is checkable without a model, because a prompt that does not contain a sentence is a text assertion.
`RS-141` and `RS-142` are the section's counter-example and they sit here on purpose: a rule that
**can** be made structural is, and those two can.

| ID | Rule | Source | Sev | Test assertion (live?) |
|----|------|--------|-----|------------------------|
| RS-135 | **A session that found no working method proposes no method.** Trials that all failed, ending with the human checking by hand, file nothing. The distillation may propose what was **ruled out** and may never propose the sequence of attempts as an approach, a workflow or a recommendation. A proposal whose own text reports that nothing worked is refused before it reaches the gate, and the refusal names this rule. | **F-35** (`background_review.py:288-296`, verbatim: *"That presents an untested sequence of failures as validated guidance a future session will trust and repeat"*); RS-109 (a lesson is something that **worked**); D6 | error | A golden session record whose enquiries all end `abandon` and whose log ends with the human reporting they checked by hand proposes zero lessons, and the run does not raise. **live**, and a **no key** twin over a scripted model instructed to propose one anyway, whose proposal is refused naming RS-135. *Why*: RS-109 files a lesson as `type.solution`, which README §1.7 and PK-10 both read as a reusable approach that worked, and PK-10 loads solution notes into every implementation pack. An untested sequence filed there is guidance every later project agent inherits, on a tree with no undo, and the session that would notice it is over. This is the sharpest of the five because the failed session is the one that feels most like it produced something. |
| RS-136 | **A failure that belonged to the environment files nothing about the domain.** A trial that failed because a device, a service, a supply or a setting was wrong on that day is logged by RS-103 and proposed as nothing. The distillation may propose a lesson about the environment when the human states it as one, in their own words, under RS-108. | **F-35**; RS-103 (the log already keeps it); RS-107 | error | A logged trial reading *"the thermometer was reading 20 low all week"* produces no proposal about the food; the same session with the human saying *"file it: check the probe against boiling water first"* proposes that, in their words. **live**, plus **no key** on the constant. *Why*: an environment-dependent failure generalises to nobody, including the human next month, and it files as a claim about the domain because the domain is what the channel is named for. The log already holds it, so nothing is lost by refusing to file it. |
| RS-137 | **A negative claim about a tool, a source or a capability files nothing.** *"Search does not return X"*, *"that site blocks us"*, *"the model cannot do Y"*: none of it becomes a note. RS-131's outcome line and the session log record what happened on the day. | **F-35**, whose own reason is the one worth keeping verbatim: *"These harden into refusals the agent cites against itself for months after the actual problem was fixed"*; RS-131; RS-132 | error | A session whose enquiry met a search outage and continued in knowledge-base mode proposes no lesson naming search; the outcome line is present in the record. **live**, plus **no key** on the constant. *Why*: RS-56 puts a filed note into the packs an expert reads on every later turn, so a note saying a capability does not work becomes a standing instruction not to try it. `F-29` guarantees this case will arise: one provider serves the models and the search, so an outage is a normal event and a session that meets one is a session with a tempting sentence to file. Hermes names the same failure and gives it the same answer. |
| RS-138 | **A failure that a retry fixed files the retry, never the failure.** An error that resolved on a second attempt proposes nothing about the error. A pattern the human states about retrying is theirs to file under RS-108. | **F-35**, verbatim: *"If retrying worked, the lesson is the retry pattern, not the original failure"*; RS-103 | warning | A logged sequence of one failure and one success on the same action proposes no lesson about the failure; a human sentence about the retry proposes as a lesson. **live**, plus **no key** on the constant. *Why*: a transient error filed as knowledge is a permanent claim about a temporary state, and the file outlives the state by design. `warning` rather than `error` because the honest boundary between a transient error and a real one is sometimes the human's call, and RS-108 already gives them the last word. |
| RS-139 | **A narrative of what this session did files nothing.** The record of one session is the session record (RS-4, RS-103), and a lesson is a claim that transfers to a session that has not happened yet. A proposal that only recounts the sequence of this channel's own work is refused. | **F-35** (one-off task narratives); RS-4; RS-102; RS-98 | error | A golden record of a nine-turn session proposes at most a claim, never a chronology; a proposal whose text is a step-by-step account of the session is refused naming RS-139; the session record still holds the account. **live**, plus **no key** on the constant. *Why*: the narrative already exists, verbatim and in the human's own words, and RS-102 hands the whole of it to the distillation. A model given a complete chronology and told to produce something will produce the chronology shorter, which reads as a lesson, files as `type.solution` and transfers to nothing. This is the failure mode `RS-140` exists to make survivable: the correct output for many sessions is nothing at all. |
| RS-140 | **The distillation carries no bias toward producing, and "nothing worth filing" is a first-class result.** No prompt in this design tells a model that a pass producing nothing is a missed opportunity, that most sessions produce something, or that proposing nothing should be rare. `propose_lessons` returning an empty tuple renders one line saying so and ends the turn. The proposal message never reports a count against an expectation. **Amended 2026-08-09 (skills): the prior binds `kind="skill"` hardest, and the amendment states it rather than leaving it to be inferred.** `F-34` is a prompt written for a system that learns **procedure**, which is exactly the kind this ruling adds, so the biasing sentences the test asserts against are the ones a skill-drafting prompt is most likely to attract. No template in `pkb.agents.research` says that a session usually produces a skill, that a good session leaves a better way of working behind it, or that skills improve through use. `C-59` carries the reconciliation. | **F-34** (`background_review.py:182-186` and `:301`, the prompt this design deliberately does not copy); decision BB; RS-105; D6 | error | An AST or text assertion over every prompt template in `pkb.agents.research` finds none of the biasing phrasings, asserted against a fixture list that includes Hermes's own two sentences so the test fails if either is pasted in; a `/learn` over a session with nothing in it renders the empty line and files nothing; the renderer has no template for "only N lessons found". **no key**. *Why*: Hermes can afford *"A pass that does nothing is a missed learning opportunity"* because its skill library carries archive, snapshots and rollback, so a bad write is reversible there. D6 says a wrong write cannot be taken back here, and RS-105 already rules that most sessions file nothing. A prompt carrying the opposite prior would fight both, and it would fight them where nobody looks: the human reads a plausible proposal and approves it, and the tree gains a note whose only cause was a sentence in a prompt. |
| RS-141 | **Nothing amends bytes it did not load in this turn.** A render that appends to an existing file reads that file's current bytes first, through the same read the human's approval diff is built from, and the rendered result is derived from those bytes. A filing whose base bytes were not loaded in the turn that produced it is refused, and the refusal names the path. The rule binds the harness renderer and the model's own `edit_file` alike: an `edit_file` on any path under `<Topic>/sessions/` or on any file carrying a recorded session origin (RS-142) is refused unless the file was read in that turn. **Amended 2026-08-09 (skills): the rule reaches `skills/**` at both scopes, and `RS-153` is its twin one step earlier.** An amendment to an existing `SKILL.md` reads that file's current bytes first, and `RS-149`'s `ORIGIN.md` is what decides whether the amendment is permitted at all. The `edit_file` arm matters more here than anywhere, because `P-53` records that the permission list already lets an expert edit its own topic's skills, so the model holds a real write tool on that path today and only the `SKILL_OVERLOAD` gate stands between it and the bytes. | **F-36** (`skill_manager_tool.py:425-451`, verbatim: *"The autonomous review fork is allowed to evolve skills, but it must not patch or rewrite content it has only inferred from the transcript"*); decision BC; RS-89 (the other half); RS-52; RS-125 | error | A second `file_research` appending to an existing file with the read stubbed out is refused and the file is byte-unchanged; the same call with the read performed lands, and the landed bytes contain every line of the base file; a planted `edit_file` on a `sessions/` path with no read in the turn is refused. **no key**. *Why*: RS-89 checks the bytes that land against the bytes the human was shown, and nothing checks the bytes those were derived from. RS-52's append and RS-125's `kind="research"` join are exactly the path where that gap opens: a renderer that reconstructs an existing file from the session record rather than from the file will drop whatever a human edited into it by hand between two enquiries, and the approval diff will look correct, because the diff is rendered from the same wrong base. Hermes states the failure in one sentence and enforces it in code rather than in its review prompt, which is RS-17's standard reached by another project independently. |
| RS-142 | **A write records its origin in the file, and nothing autonomous amends a file it did not write.** Every filing appends a block to `## Sessions` naming the session id, the thread id and the date, written by code and read back by code, which makes it the origin tag rather than prose. A file carrying no such block is human content and every autonomous amendment path refuses it. `resolve_note_path(kind="research")` narrows accordingly: joining an existing file needs a matching recorded `## Question` **and** a recorded origin naming this thread, and it refuses otherwise (RS-125 as amended). `provenance` does **not** carry this: it names the route the content took and RS-48 permits exactly one comparison site over its values, so authorship needs its own record. **Amended 2026-08-09 (skills): the skill class records its origin in a sibling file, and `RS-149` is that rule.** A `SKILL.md` carries no PKB frontmatter (`VA-6`) and its body is loaded into a model's prompt, so neither `provenance` nor a `## Sessions` block can live there: the frontmatter refuses the first, and the second would arrive in a prompt as text a model reads as procedure. `<skill-folder>/ORIGIN.md` is the same mechanism at the same reliability, written by code and read back by code, and executed on a fixture tree it validates clean, leaves `check_skill_dir` empty and reaches no index and no tag artifact (`P-55`). The boundary this rule draws is unchanged: a folder with no `ORIGIN.md` is the human's. | **F-36** (`skill_provenance.py`, verbatim: *"Skills a user asks a foreground agent to write belong to the user and must never be auto-curated"*); decision BC; RS-48; RS-52; RS-125; `ingestion._recorded_origin` (the built precedent: a code-written block read back by code) | error | A hand-written `<Topic>/sessions/notes-on-x.md` with no `## Sessions` block refuses every append and is byte-identical afterwards; a file this thread filed accepts the append; a file another thread filed with the same `## Question` is refused naming the owning session; the block is written by code and a planted model write to it is refused (RS-82's shape on the knowledge-base side is RS-141's read requirement plus the gate). **no key**. *Why*: RS-40 already forbids this loop touching the human's notes and RS-125 already refuses a taken lesson path, so the tree is protected on two paths and unprotected on the third, which is the research file the loop is allowed to append to. Hermes reached the same boundary from the other side and drew it in the same place: what a human asked for is theirs, and autonomous curation touches only what the autonomous process made. The knowledge base's version is stricter for a reason D6 supplies, since a wrong append here is unrecoverable and Hermes has snapshots. |

### 1.14 The skill a session may propose (RS)

*Added 2026-08-09 (skills), from the human's ruling on `Q54`. Decision `BD` carries the ruling, `BE`
the gate and the writer, `BF` the provenance value. `P-53 … P-58` carry the grounding, and the shape
to hold in mind is that **most of this is built**: the placement rule is `resolve_skills`, the file
class is `VA-6`, the exclusion from every artifact is `GE-15`, the gate reason is `SKILL_OVERLOAD`,
and the write permission is the topic expert's own subtree allow. This section adds a tool, a proposal
kind, a path resolver, an origin record and the guards that keep a skill from becoming the one file
class the human never approved.*

**Why a skill needs its own rules when a lesson already has some.** A wrong note is one file a reader
may never open. A wrong skill is procedure loaded into every later prompt in its scope, before
anybody asks a question, and `expert.base_skill_sources` puts the root's `skills/` in front of every
expert in the tree. `D6` says neither can be taken back. So every rule below is the lesson rule with the blast
radius raised, and `RS-151` is where that difference is stated rather than implied.

| ID | Rule | Source | Sev | Test assertion (live?) |
|----|------|--------|-----|------------------------|
| RS-143 | **`/learn` may propose a skill, and the proposal says which it is.** `Proposal.kind` gains `"skill"` beside `"lesson"` and `"summary"`, and the member is what the proposal **files as** rather than what the session was (RS-98's wording, unchanged). A `/learn` run proposes any mix of the three, each with its own id under `RS-11`'s shape, and the human answers all of them through `RS-121`'s one grammar. `LEARN_MAX_PROPOSALS` bounds the set whole rather than per kind. | decision BD (the human's ruling on Q54); RS-100; RS-113; RS-118; §5.2 | error | A `/learn` producing one lesson and one skill renders two ids in one message and files each through its own gate; `Proposal.kind` has exactly three members; `keep p1 p2` files one note and one skill; the cap counts across kinds. **no key**. *Why*: `Q54` encoded its own default structurally, as a two-member `Literal`, so the ruling is one member and the rules that member drags behind it. Keeping one proposal set rather than two is what keeps `RS-121`'s grammar the only thing a human has to learn: they answer *"keep p1, drop p2"* without knowing that `p2` was going to change how the expert works. |
| RS-144 | **A note says what is true; a skill says how to work.** That sentence is the test, and it is applied in one direction: a proposal whose text would still be true with no agent anywhere in the system is a **note**, and a proposal that only means something as an instruction to an agent is a **skill**. *"Brisket holds at 250 for four hours"* is a note. *"Before proposing a rub change, check the last three trials for the temperature they ran at"* is a skill. The distillation picks the kind, code renders it, and a proposal whose kind disagrees with its own text is refused before the gate with both the kind and the sentence named. | decision BD; `F-37` (Hermes learns how to work, this project learns what is true); README §1.3's file-class table; RS-109 | error | A golden proposal stating a subject fact with `kind="skill"` is refused naming RS-144 and quoting the sentence; the mirror case with `kind="lesson"` over an instruction is refused the same way; the refusal happens before any path is resolved and before any gate is raised. **no key**. *Why*: the line gets tested at every `/learn`, so it has to be a sentence the human and the model apply the same way, and *"is this procedural?"* is not one. The counterfactual test is, because it turns a judgement about a category into a question about a sentence. Getting it wrong in the note direction files an instruction into `notes/`, where `PK-10` loads it into every implementation pack as a `type.solution` claim about the subject. Getting it wrong in the skill direction puts a fact into a file that is read as a procedure, on every later turn, by a model that will try to follow it. |
| RS-145 | **Placement follows specificity, and the scope is a field on the proposal.** `Proposal.scope` is `Literal["topic", "root"]`. A `topic` skill lands at `<Topic>/skills/<name>/SKILL.md`, inside the expert's own subtree. A `root` skill lands at `<kb>/skills/<name>/SKILL.md`. `resolve_skills` already merges the root's entries under each topic's and lets the topic win, so a root skill reaches every expert and a topic skill of the same name overrides it for that topic alone. **The packaged mount is a third source and `resolve_skills` does not see it** (`P-58`): the shipped skills are laid underneath both by `expert.base_skill_sources`, which is why `RS-150` compares a proposed name against `DEFAULT_SKILL_NAMES` **and** against `resolve_skills` rather than against one of them. **No new resolution mechanism, no new file class and no Layer 1 change.** | the human's ruling (*"the topic if it is topic specific, or in librarian root if it is process specific"*); **`P-56`** (executed: `paths.py:572-574`); `PA-14`; `VA-6`; `GE-15` | error | A `topic`-scoped filing lands under the expert's topic root and resolves for that topic and no other; a `root`-scoped filing resolves for every topic; a root skill and a topic skill sharing a name resolve to the topic's for that topic and to the root's everywhere else, asserted through `pkb.core.resolve_skills` rather than through a re-implementation; both validate with zero findings and appear in no index and in no tag artifact. **no key**. *Why*: the human's rule and the schema's rule are the same rule, and the whole risk here is inventing a second one. `resolve_skills` merges root under topic with the topic winning, `SKILLS_DIR` sits in the topic folder set and in `_NO_DESCENT`, `VA-6` makes `skills/**` a third file class validated for `name` and `description` alone, and `GE-15` keeps it out of the topic index. Executed on a fixture tree, a topic skill validates clean, appears in neither the topic index nor the root registry, and wins the merge (`P-55`, `P-56`). |
| RS-146 | **Filing a skill gates, through a third tool with a third reason.** `file_skill(session_id, proposal_id)` carries no content and no path, exactly as `file_lesson` does. `GateReason.SKILL_PROPOSAL` is keyed by that tool, carries `("approve", "reject")`, and shares `GateEnv.research_render`. **`GateReason.SKILL_OVERLOAD`'s path branch is untouched** and keeps firing on a model's own `write_file` into any `skills/` folder, which `RS-147` shows is a reachable call today. | RS-50, RS-87, RS-88, RS-116; **`P-54`** (executed: `requires_approval` returns `skill-overload`); RT-29; RT-65 (amended) | error | `file_skill`'s schema exposes exactly two string parameters; every call gates with `SKILL_PROPOSAL`; `allowed_decisions("file_skill")` is `("approve", "reject")`; the bytes on disk equal the bytes the gate described, including the shadow notice of RS-150; a planted `write_file` to a `skills/` path still gates with `SKILL_OVERLOAD` and is not swallowed by the new branch. **no key**. *Why*: the gate question answers itself from the built table, since a write under any `skills/` folder gates today, so the human already agreed a skill is worth stopping for. A third **reason** buys the framing `RS-116` argues for: reusing `SESSION_LESSON` would put *"a lesson is ready to file"* in front of a file that changes how the expert works on every later turn, which is the one thing about it the human most needs to read. The friction objection is answered by the same argument in reverse: the human agreed to a `/learn` and to a proposal, and they have not yet agreed to these bytes. `RS-89`'s re-render check applies unchanged, so approving them is approving what lands. A path-keyed branch cannot serve this, because `RT-65` puts the tool-keyed branches ahead of every path question and a content-free call carries no path to key on. |
| RS-147 | **The permission list does not change, and here is what it already allows and still denies.** Executed against `deepagents.middleware.filesystem._check_fs_permission` with `kb_permissions("Cooking")`: `/kb/Cooking/skills/reverse-sear/SKILL.md` is **allow**, `/kb/Physics/skills/x/SKILL.md` is **deny**, `/kb/skills/research/SKILL.md` is **deny**, and `/skills/research/SKILL.md` is **deny**. For the Librarian (`topic_path=None`) every one of the four is deny. `SKILLS_DENY_GLOBS` is `("/skills/**",)` and it governs the **packaged mount** alone; it neither moves nor narrows. The four facts stay true after this feature ships, and the acceptance test asserts all four rather than the one this design uses. | **`P-53`** (executed: `permissions.py:110`, `permissions.py:158-171`, `paths.py:55`); RT-15; RT-16; RT-17; `paths.py`'s `SKILLS_MOUNT` | error | The four paths above return their four modes for an expert and four denies for the Librarian, asserted through the harness's own checker rather than through a glob reading; the assertion runs before and after the feature and neither result changes; `SKILLS_DENY_GLOBS` is asserted equal to `(SKILLS_MOUNT + "**",)` so a later widening to `/kb/**/skills/**` fails the test. **no key**. *Why*: this rule exists because the brief for this feature stated the opposite, and stated it as the thing that had to change: *"every agent is denied every skill write, before its own-subtree allow is even considered."* Read that way, the Layer 2 work is a permission edit, and a permission edit made on a wrong reading of a first-match-wins list is how an expert quietly gains write access to a neighbour's tree. The list already draws every line this feature needs. Writing the four answers down, with the checker that produced them, is what stops the next reader making the same edit for the same reason. |
| RS-148 | **A root process skill is written by harness code, and the Librarian gains no writer.** The expert that ran the session holds `file_skill` and calls it for both scopes. For `scope="root"` the write is performed by `research.py:_file_skill`, which becomes RT-18's **fifth** sanctioned harness writer with the same two obligations as the other four: validate before writing, and record into `kb_touched`. The model holds no tool whose path reaches outside its own subtree, so `RT-15` is untouched at the permission layer and untouched in fact. | decision BE; `RT-16`; **`RT-18`** and `create_topic`'s precedent; `adopt_skill` (built: harness code writing `<kb>/skills/<name>/` outside the permission layer); RS-112; RS-119 | error | A `root`-scoped filing lands at `<kb>/skills/<name>/SKILL.md` after approval, with `kb_touched` naming it so MW-18's flush runs; the expert's own `write_file` to the same path is still denied by the permission list (RS-147); RT-18's allow-list test asserts a five-element set; no `file_skill` tool is bound to the Librarian's graph, asserted over a built registry. **no key**. *Why*: `RT-16` gives one reason for the Librarian not writing, and the reason is about a **note**: *"Filing a note needs the topic's skills, voice overload and `expert.md` behaviour, none of which the Librarian loads."* A process skill needs none of the three, so that reasoning does not reach it and the rule refuses the Librarian on two other grounds instead. `RS-112` says the Librarian writes nothing and every guarantee in §1.11 rests on it, and `RS-119`'s fan-out means an expert authored the proposal even on a Librarian channel, so there is never a proposal only the Librarian could file. Handing the expert a **write permission** at root was the other option and it is refused outright: it widens `RT-15` for every turn to serve one gated filing, and `RT-15` is the rule that makes *"a solution note lives in exactly one topic"* mechanical. |
| RS-149 | **A session amends only a skill carrying its own recorded origin, and the origin never sits inside `SKILL.md`.** A filing writes `<skill-folder>/ORIGIN.md`, holding the session id, the thread id, the scope and the date, written by code and read back by code, which makes it the origin record rather than prose. A skill folder with no `ORIGIN.md` is the human's and every autonomous amendment path refuses it, naming the folder. This is `RS-142`'s rule for the skill class, and the placement differs for one reason: deepagents loads a `SKILL.md` body into a model's prompt, so an origin block written there becomes text a model reads as procedure. | decision BC; `RS-142`; `RS-141`; **`F-36`** (`skill_provenance.py`: *"Skills a user asks a foreground agent to write belong to the user and must never be auto-curated"*); README §2.4 (*"Anything else the skill needs sits beside its `SKILL.md` in the same folder"*); `VA-6` | error | A hand-written `<Topic>/skills/x/` with no `ORIGIN.md` refuses every amendment and is byte-identical afterwards; a folder this session filed accepts one; an `ORIGIN.md` beside a `SKILL.md` validates with zero findings, leaves `check_skill_dir` empty, appears in no index and contributes no tags, asserted over a real fixture tree; the amended `SKILL.md` body contains no origin block, asserted as a text absence. **no key**. *Why*: the question the human left open is whether a session may revise a skill or only add one, and the honest answer is **both, bounded by who wrote it**. `F-36` is the same boundary Hermes drew from the other side, and `D6` makes this project's version stricter, since a wrong amendment here has no rollback and Hermes has snapshots. Putting the record in a sibling file rather than in the frontmatter is forced by `VA-6`: a `SKILL.md` carries the harness's two fields and no PKB frontmatter, so `provenance` cannot ride there and neither can `## Sessions`. Executed on a fixture tree, a sibling markdown file inside a skill folder validates clean, is recorded by the scan and is excluded from every artifact (`P-55`). |
| RS-150 | **A proposed name that shadows a shipped skill is named three times and refused none of them.** The name that decides is the rendered file's **frontmatter** `name`, since deepagents keys its merge on that field (`P-58`), and `RS-152` is what keeps it equal to the folder name. Code compares it against `DEFAULT_SKILL_NAMES` for the packaged mount and against `resolve_skills(kb_root, topic)` for the tree, which are two lookups because they are two sources. A collision renders its own marked line in the proposal message, appears again in the gate's description, and is written **into** the file as its first body line, reusing `adopt_skill`'s own fork notice rather than a second copy of it. The three cases are distinct and the message says which one holds: a new name shadows nothing, a shipped name shadows the mount permanently, and an existing file at the resolved path is an amendment governed by `RS-149`. | `SK-4`, **`SK-5`** (the notice is written into the copy *and* returned, because whoever ran the command will not be the one who opens the file six months later); `adopt_skill` (built); `RS-124`'s two-chance shape for an unregistered tag; `P-56` | error | A proposal named `research` renders the shadow line in the message and in the gate, and the landed file's first body line is the fork notice naming the shipped skill; a proposal named `reverse-sear` renders no such line anywhere; a proposal resolving onto an existing file takes `RS-149`'s path instead and never renders a shadow line; the notice text comes from one function, asserted by a single call site. **no key**. *Why*: the merge is silent and correct at both seams, so the shipped `research` skill stops being read the moment a file declaring `name: research` lands, and nothing anywhere says so. `resolve_skills` is the tree's half and deepagents' last-source-wins is the packaged half, and neither one logs, indexes or tags the swap. That is exactly how behaviour changes for a reason nobody can find later. Refusing the collision is the wrong answer, because improving the shipped skill for one topic is the most obvious thing a human would ever want here and `SK-4` already blesses it. Two renderings plus the file itself is `RS-124`'s reasoning applied to a bigger consequence: a line inside a diff is a thing a human skims, and a file they open in six months is the only reader that matters. |
| RS-151 | **§1.13's exclusions bind a skill proposal, and the bar is higher.** `RS-135 … RS-140` apply to `kind="skill"` word for word, and `RS-135` binds hardest: a session that found no working method proposes no skill, because a skill is exactly the shape an untested sequence of failures takes when it is written up. `LEARN_EXCLUSIONS` reaches the distillation once and covers all three kinds. A `root`-scoped proposal states, in the proposal message, that it changes every expert in the tree. | `RS-135 … RS-140`; decision BB; `F-35`; `D6`; `RS-145` | error | The golden record whose enquiries all end `abandon` proposes zero skills as well as zero lessons; a `root`-scoped proposal renders the scope line naming every expert; the exclusion constant reaches the distillation context byte for byte for all three kinds, asserted once. **live**, plus a **no key** twin on the constant and on the scope line. *Why*: `RS-135`'s own reasoning is about guidance a future session trusts and repeats, and a skill is the file the system trusts and repeats **without being asked**, since `topic_skill_sources` puts it in front of the model before any question arrives. `RS-140`'s inverted prior matters more here than anywhere: a model that produces a plausible skill from a session that learned nothing has written procedure the human approves once and then never sees again. The scope line exists because *"this changes how every expert works"* is a fact about the file that its own text never states. |
| RS-152 | **A skill that would never load is refused before the gate.** `pkb.agents.skills.check_skill_dir` runs on the rendered folder, and a `SKILL_NAME_MISMATCH` or an `INERT_SKILL_OVERLOAD` finding refuses the filing with the finding's own message. `pkb.core.validate_content` runs first, as `RS-20`'s order requires, so a missing `name` or `description` is a `VA-6` error and never reaches the gate either. | **`SK-15`** (built: the two resolution failures Layer 1 cannot see); `VA-6`; `RS-20`; `SK-16` | error | A rendered skill whose frontmatter `name` differs from its folder name is refused naming `SKILL_NAME_MISMATCH`; a `root` filing whose holder is neither the knowledge-base root nor a topic root is refused naming `INERT_SKILL_OVERLOAD`; a skill missing `description` is refused by `VA-6` before the gate; the tree is byte-identical after each refusal. **no key**. *Why*: both failures are silent by construction, and the name one fails in two directions rather than one (`P-58`). deepagents keys its merge on the frontmatter `name`, logs a warning nobody reads and loads the skill anyway, so a folder named `research` declaring `name: my-research` leaves the shipped `research` in force under a file the human approved, and a folder named `my-research` declaring `name: research` shadows the shipped skill from a directory entry that says otherwise. Both sit on disk looking correct. `SK-15` exists because Layer 1 cannot see either one, and the check is built and unused on this path. Asking a human to approve bytes that will never be read is worse than refusing them, because the refusal is fixable in the same turn. |
| RS-153 | **A proposal that names an existing file quotes that file's current bytes, read in the same `/learn` run.** `propose_lessons` may not describe, amend or supersede a file it has not loaded in that run, and a proposal citing a path with no matching read is refused before it is rendered, naming the path. This is `RS-141` one step earlier: `RS-141` binds the **renderer** and this binds the **proposal**, so the human never reads a proposed revision derived from a file's remembered contents. | **`F-36`** (`skill_manager_tool.py:425-451`, the same guard enforced in code: *"it must not patch or rewrite content it has only inferred from the transcript"*); decision BC; `RS-141`; `RS-102`; README §2.8 (which asserted this and carried no rule id) | error | A proposal naming an existing note with the read stubbed out is refused and nothing is rendered; the same proposal with the read performed renders and quotes the file; the quoted text is asserted byte-identical to the file; `RS-141` still refuses at the render step when the proposal passed, so the two guards are asserted independently rather than through each other. **no key**. *Why*: the README states this and no rule carried it, which makes it advice. The gap it closes is narrow and real: `RS-141` checks the bytes an amendment is **derived from**, and a proposal is what the human reads before any amendment exists. A proposal reading *"your March note says sugar burns at 250, and trial nine says otherwise"* is a claim about a file, and a model that reconstructs that claim from a compacted conversation will get the March note wrong in a message the human has no reason to check. Its live callers are `RS-52`'s research-file append and `RS-149`'s skill amendment, because `RS-125` refuses a taken lesson path outright, and stating that is what stops a reader believing `/learn` revises notes today. |

## 2. Where the docs and the built code are wrong

Rows `P-34 … P-46` were executed on 2026-08-09 during the grounding workflows, against this repo and,
where stated, the live internet. `P-47` was executed while writing this document. `P-48 … P-52` were executed during the search
settlement and `P-53 … P-58` during the skills amendment, both in this repo's venv on the same day.
Layers 1 to 5's `P-1 … P-33` are unchanged and still hold.

| # | Doc says | What the built code actually does | Corrected approach |
|---|----------|-----------------------------------|--------------------|
| **P-34** | Research sub-agents are the natural deepagents shape (the human's design point 1). | **A `task` delegation is the bet this repo lost one layer up.** `routing.py`'s docstring records the measurement: the Librarian held `task`, did not delegate, answered from raw files, and once claimed an expert had run when none had. | Decision AM / RS-15: the fan-out is code under a semaphore, the researcher has no agent id, and `task` appears nowhere in either new module. |
| **P-35** | The checkpoint is a human-in-the-loop approval. | **An approval blocks every other message on its thread with a 409** (RT-39 → `ApprovalPendingError`), so a mixed answer is not expressible. | Decision AN / RS-8: the checkpoint ends the turn; the session lives in the thread's scratch filesystem; the human's next ordinary message resumes it through one typed call. |
| **P-36** | The `type.*` tag distinguishes a web result from a book. | **It restates the path.** VA-13 and VA-14 force the declared type to agree with the folder, so `type.*` carries no information the path does not. `trust.*` is refused by the closed namespace set (`UNKNOWN_TAG_NAMESPACE`/TG-2) and `source_type: clipping` by the closed authored set (`UNKNOWN_SOURCE_TYPE`/FM-6). `KNOWN_FIELDS` is the seven required plus `last_reviewed`, `related_topics` and `review_note`, so no provenance field exists; provenance lives in the body under `## Provenance` and `_recorded_origin` reads it back, which means the fact survives and no generator can act on it. | Decision AQ / RS-42: a recognised optional field with a closed vocabulary, landing as `FM-16` and `VA-42` in the Layer 1 rules. |
| **P-37** | An unknown frontmatter key is preserved, so provenance can ride in an ad-hoc key. | **VA-32 advises removing it.** Unknown keys are preserved with a warning whose hint reads *"Check for a typo of a known field (title, description, topic, tags, …); the key is preserved either way"*, and warnings ride along on the success message the model sees. Adding a recognised optional field is five places and one test. | RS-43 and RS-44: the field becomes known before any writer emits it, and VA-32's hint gains a clause forbidding removal of a key the model did not add. |
| **P-38** | `CLAUDE.md` and the README treat a URL as a second-class inbound. | **A URL is already a first-class source.** `ingest_source(origin)` states path-or-URL in its own description, and an executed run fetched a page, staged it with a manifest and a digest, extracted title, author, date and site, walked its sections, wrote `references/<slug>/<slug>.md` with a `## Provenance` block, copied the original beside it, and wrote a `## Reading record`. `validate_tree` returned zero errors. | RS-55: the accepted-source outcome is one `ingest_source` call. The research loop writes no reference file of its own. |
| **P-39** | The large-source spec header and `CLAUDE.md` said large-source ingestion was designed, not built. | **It is built**, about 3,900 lines. Corrected on this branch's parent. | Nothing to do here beyond citing it: the chunked loop, the reading record and the reconciliation are available to this design rather than owed by it. |
| **P-40** | A second clip from a URL needs a new source type. | **A second clip reconciles into pass 2, un-gated.** "Clip another paragraph" is already a second reading of one source. | Nothing new. RS-55 inherits it, and `source_type: clipping` stays refused (FM-6). |
| **P-41** | A goal needs a durable home, so it needs a table or a KB file. | **A durable home exists and nothing uses it**: the thread's own `StateBackend` scratch filesystem. A probe wrote `/goal.md`, `kill -9`'d the daemon, restarted, and read it back byte for byte. No KB write, no gate, no schema change; deepagents uses the same route. | RS-4: `/sessions/<session-id>.md` in scratch. No new table, no `pkb.service` change, no Layer 3 work at all. |
| **P-42** | LS-13 to LS-16 describe the ingestion loop's quotation check. | **They are specified and not implemented.** A probe fed the loop a fabricated quotation about a section the model had never seen; it was filed verbatim with `gainful: True, findings: ()`. | RS-28 depends on LS-13's check existing, so §8 increment 1 implements LS-13 first. Reuse, never a second copy. |
| **P-43** | The packs implement README §1.7's "human content always wins". | **They invert it.** An implementation pack carries every reference in full and gives the human `notes/summary.md` plus `type.solution` notes only; a plain `type.note` reaches neither pack. | RS-0a and RS-56: notes first, then provenance ordering inside each role group. The amendment lives in PK-9 and PK-10. |
| **P-44** | An AI-authored note is a draft awaiting approval. | **It lands unattended.** `requires_approval` returns `None` for `<Topic>/notes/<anything>.md`; it arrives `status.draft` and nothing in Layers 3 or 4 surfaces a queue of drafts. **Scope, read from the chain**: `_is_curated` covers extension-folder content and `_introduces_approved` fires on a brand-new file carrying `status.approved`, so a research file tagged `status.approved` already gates today. What does not gate is a new curated file carrying `status.draft` or `status.conflict-review` with no new tags, which is the case RS-51 and RS-50 are about. | RS-50 gives research output its own gate reason, keyed by the tool. RS-95 closes the notes path for the turns this feature opens. The wider hole (any AI-authored note, on any turn) is named in §7 as out of scope and worth its own rule. |
| **P-45** | `/health` reports the conflict-scan worker's state. | **The worker can never start**: `build_app` never passes a `scan_queue`, so `/health` reports it disabled forever. Same empty-slot bug as the Telegram task before it was wired. | Not this spec's defect, and it matters here: RS-40 leaves a filed reference to the ordinary scan, and the ordinary scan does not run. Named in §7 with the fix's owner. |
| **P-46** | The MCP mount means the system speaks MCP. | **Only the serving half exists.** No MCP client exists anywhere; the daemon serves MCP and nothing consumes it. And no web search of any kind exists. | §1.8 builds the consuming half. §8 orders it after the KB-only increment so nothing waits on a vendor. **Amended 2026-08-09 (search settled)**: the second sentence stands and its consequence changed. No web search exists in *this repo*, and one exists in a package the venv already holds (`P-50`). §1.8 still builds the MCP half and search stops depending on it, so increment 4 no longer waits on increment 6. |
| **P-47** | (not in any doc) | **Executed here**: `mcp` is importable in this venv; `langchain_mcp_adapters`, `duckduckgo_search` and `tavily` are not. `httpx` is importable and declared. | Q37 rules the client dependency with a recommended default; RS-77's day-one increment needs none of them. |
| **P-48** | The human's design point and this document's own §1.8 assumed a search tool arrives from somewhere: deepagents, a vendor SDK, or an MCP server. | **deepagents 0.7.5 ships no search tool.** Method, re-runnable: `grep -rliE` over the installed `deepagents` for the alternation of `tavily`, `websearch`, `web_search`, `internet_search`, `duckduckgo` and `serper` returns no matches. Its exports are harness machinery: `create_deep_agent`, `FilesystemMiddleware`, `SubAgent`, `SubAgentMiddleware`, `MemoryMiddleware`, `RubricMiddleware`, profiles, backends. The filesystem tools every expert already holds are what it ships. | Nothing to change in the design and one assumption to delete: deepagents is a harness rather than a toolbox, so no increment can get search by upgrading it. RS-128 supplies the tool. |
| **P-49** | deepagents' own examples show `internet_search = {"google_search": {}}`, which reads as a portable way to give an agent search. | **It is a passthrough to the model provider, and it fails at bind time on Ollama.** deepagents does not interpret it: `_tools.py:_tool_name` reads `tool.get("name")` and forwards the rest, so the provider executes the search server-side. Executed: `ChatOllama(model="deepseek-v4-flash:cloud").bind_tools([{"google_search": {}}])` raises `ValueError: Unsupported function {'google_search': {}}` before any request leaves the process. | The example works on a Gemini model because Google runs it. RS-128 calls the provider's search API directly instead. `P-49` is also RS-76's source for the shape of a credential error: this provider raises `ValueError` from client code rather than returning a status. |
| **P-50** | This document's Q38 treated web search as a vendor choice with a key and a bill attached. | **The model provider ships search and its client is in the venv.** Executed: `ollama.web_search(query: str, max_results: int = 3)` and `ollama.web_fetch(url: str)` are exported by `ollama` 0.6.2, which is installed and required by `langchain-ollama`. Its own requirements are `httpx` and `pydantic`, both already declared, so naming it a direct dependency adds one line to `pyproject.toml` and zero packages to the closure. | Q38 resolves to the provider's client, decision AR narrows, and RS-128 and RS-129 rule the two tools. Q37's dependency measurement is satisfied here rather than deferred: the closure adds nothing and pulls no second HTTP stack. |
| **P-51** | (not in any doc) | **The response shapes, read from the installed client.** `WebSearchResponse.results: Sequence[WebSearchResult]`; `WebSearchResult(title, url, content)` with **every field optional and defaulting to `None`**; `WebFetchResponse(title, content, links)` on the same terms. Against the live internet on 2026-08-09, `content` measured 8,701 to 22,448 characters per result and `web_fetch` returned 17,591 characters and 3 links, both in under a second. `max_results` defaults to 3. | RS-127's dropped-result clause and RS-128's drop-and-count clause exist because an absent `content` is a **legal** response rather than an impossible one. RS-68's default of 3 follows the client's own. `F-27` carries the measurement. |
| **P-52** | TG-24's shape assumes a secret can be handed to a library as an argument, and the obvious call `ollama.web_search(...)` is a module-level function. | **The module-level function reads the key from the environment inside the library**, raising `ValueError: Authorization header with Bearer token is required for web search` with no key set. Executed, the injected form works: `ollama.AsyncClient(headers={"Authorization": f"Bearer {key}"})` carries the credential, exposes `web_search` and `web_fetch`, and both are coroutines, so the async research loop calls them with no thread offload. | RS-133 forbids the module-level call and requires the injected client, which is what keeps `OLLAMA_API_KEY` inside `pkb.daemon` and lets RS-134's module scan mean something. The async surface is why RS-60's sync-versus-async problem does not recur here. |
| **P-53** | This feature's brief, and every reading of `permissions.py` that stops at the second rule: *"every agent is denied every skill write, before its own-subtree allow is even considered. An expert therefore cannot write `<its own topic>/skills/**` today, deliberately."* | **The deny is the packaged mount and an expert already writes its own topic's skills.** `SKILLS_DENY_GLOBS` is `(SKILLS_MOUNT + "**",)` (`permissions.py:110`), `SKILLS_MOUNT` is `"/skills/"` (`paths.py:55`), and the knowledge base is mounted at `/kb/` (`paths.py:48`), so the two are different routes and the deny never sees a knowledge-base path. Executed through the harness's own checker, `_check_fs_permission(kb_permissions("Cooking"), "write", path)`: `/kb/Cooking/skills/reverse-sear/SKILL.md` → **allow**; `/kb/Physics/skills/x/SKILL.md` → **deny**; `/kb/skills/research/SKILL.md` → **deny**; `/skills/research/SKILL.md` → **deny**. With `topic_path=None` all four are **deny**. | RS-147: nothing narrows and no glob moves. The Layer 2 change is a tool, a gate reason and a fifth entry on RT-18's allow-list (RS-146, RS-148). Recorded as a finding rather than fixed quietly, because the wrong reading points at a permission edit and a permission edit on a first-match-wins list is how an expert gains a neighbour's tree. |
| **P-54** | `Q54` and §7 read as though a written skill would need a gate designed for it. | **The gate is built and it fires today.** Executed on a scaffolded fixture: `requires_approval("write_file", "Cooking/skills/reverse-sear/SKILL.md", …, snapshot)` returns `skill-overload`, and so does the same call for `skills/research/SKILL.md` at the root. `GateReason.SKILL_OVERLOAD` (`gates.py:160`) is documented as *"Anything under a `skills/` directory, topic-level or KB-root (RT-29)"*, `_is_skill_path` (`gates.py:530-538`) carries both arms, and `GATE_DECISIONS` gives it `WRITE_DECISIONS`. For contrast in the same run, `Cooking/notes/x.md` returns `None` and `Cooking/sessions/s.md` returns `extension-folder`. | RS-146 keeps that branch untouched and adds a **tool-keyed** reason beside it, because RT-65 puts tool branches ahead of every path question and `file_skill` carries no path. The gate question is therefore settled by what the human already agreed to rather than by an argument about friction. |
| **P-55** | `Q54` names `SK-17`'s exclusion of `skills/**` from every index and tag artifact as an obstacle: *"a self-written skill is invisible to everything this document uses to find things."* | **The exclusion is the file class working, and a skill is found by walking directories.** Executed on a fixture tree holding `Cooking/skills/research/`, `Cooking/skills/reverse-sear/` and `skills/research/`: `validate_content` on a `SKILL.md` carrying only `name` and `description` returns `[]`, and the same file missing `description` returns `MISSING_SKILL_FIELD error VA-6`. `render_topic_index(snapshot, "Cooking")` lists the three breadth files and no skill. `render_root_tags(snapshot)` carries `topic.cooking`, `type`, `status` and nothing from any skill. `validate_tree` over the whole fixture, including an `ORIGIN.md` beside a `SKILL.md`, returns zero findings. | RS-145 and RS-149: no Layer 1 change is needed to validate, index, tag or record a written skill, and the sibling origin file is legal on the same terms. Invisibility to the index is not invisibility to an expert, because `PA-14`'s resolution reads directories rather than artifacts. |
| **P-56** | The human's placement rule reads like a mechanism this design would have to build. | **It is `resolve_skills`, built and byte-exact.** `paths.py:572-574` seeds the merge with the root's `skills/` and then applies each topic root on the chain, outermost first, so the topic wins. Executed on the fixture above, `resolve_skills(root, root/"Cooking")` returns `{'research': 'Cooking/skills/research/SKILL.md', 'reverse-sear': 'Cooking/skills/reverse-sear/SKILL.md'}`: the root's `research` is shadowed for Cooking and unchanged for every other topic. `SKILLS_DIR` sits in the topic folder set and in `_NO_DESCENT` (`paths.py:135`). | RS-145 spends one `scope` field and cites this function. RS-150 renders the shadowing consequence, because the merge is silent by design and correct by design, and both facts are what make a collision unfindable later. |
| **P-57** | README §2.4 read the shipped `research` skill as covering the internet rounds and the `/learn` proposal. | **It covers neither.** Read from `src/pkb/agents/skills/research/SKILL.md`: its description is *"exploring a question breadth-first across the knowledge base"*, its four sections are what to read inside the tree, contested knowledge, an enumerated option set and an approval gate, and line 59 says *"Research files nothing on its own."* No sentence mentions a search, a URL, a round, a session or `/learn`. | `C-38` already required the split and this row is its evidence. §8 accounts for the three skill files this document owes: **research planning and synthesis** in increment 4, **lesson proposal** in increment 2a, and **skill proposal** in increment 2c. |
| **P-58** | Both documents written on the ruling said a knowledge-base skill shadows a **shipped** skill through `pkb.core.resolve_skills`, and `P-56` was read as covering that case. | **`resolve_skills` never sees a shipped skill, and the shipped shadow is a second mechanism.** Executed on a fixture holding `skills/research/` and `Cooking/skills/reverse-sear/`: `resolve_skills(root, root/"Cooking")` returns those two files and nothing from `packaged_skills_root()`, which is `src/pkb/agents/skills/` inside the installed package. The packaged mount is laid underneath by `expert.base_skill_sources` (`expert.py:260-262`), which returns `["/skills/", "/kb/skills/"]`, and `topic_skill_sources` appends each topic root after it. deepagents merges the sources last-wins in `middleware/skills.py:961`, `all_skills[skill["name"]] = skill`, so the key is the **frontmatter** `name`. `_validate_skill_name` (`skills.py:347`) only warns when that name differs from the folder, and the skill still loads. | `RS-145` names the third source, `RS-150` keeps its two lookups, and `RS-152` becomes load-bearing rather than tidy: a folder named `research` declaring `name: my-research` shadows nothing, and a folder named `my-research` declaring `name: research` shadows the shipped skill. Both look right in a directory listing and both are silent, and `check_skill_dir`'s `SKILL_NAME_MISMATCH` is the only thing that sees either one. `SK-16` is where the two resolvers are pinned to agree, and it agrees on the tree's half alone by construction. |

---

## 3. Contradictions with existing rules, and how each resolves

| # | Contradiction | Resolution | Why |
|---|---------------|------------|-----|
| **C-38** | **The shipped `research` skill says "Research files nothing on its own"** (`skills/research/SKILL.md:59`), and ends with an explicit request for direction. This design files a gated research file and records declined sources. | **Amend the skill in the same commit**, and split it explicitly into its two procedures so one file does not carry two meanings of the word: *breadth-first exploration inside the tree*, which still files nothing, and *a goal-driven session that reaches past the tree*, whose outcomes are RS-49 and RS-55. `tests/agents/test_shipped_skills.py` moves in the same commit. And rule the deeper point: **no guarantee in this spec is carried by a skill.** **Amended 2026-08-09 (skills): the split's second half is a skill file this document owes, and `P-57` is the evidence the gap is real.** Read from `src/pkb/agents/skills/research/SKILL.md`, the shipped text covers the breadth-first pass over the tree and nothing else: no search, no URL, no round, no session, no `/learn`. So the amendment is not one file re-worded into two meanings, it is one file narrowed to the meaning it already has plus a second file that does not exist. §8 places it: **research planning and synthesis** in increment 4, where the rounds exist, in the same commit as the narrowing. The deeper point stands and `C-56` restates it against the human's ruling that a session may now write skills. | Adoption is a permanent fork: a human who has adopted `skills/research/` keeps their copy forever and later improvements stop reaching it. A guarantee that lives in skill text is a guarantee that silently disappears the day someone edits their copy, and the skill is exactly the file a human editing their own workflow reaches for first. |
| **C-39** | **RT-18's corollary says no other `pkb.agents` code writes under `kb_root`**, and it names an allow-list of three with an AST test over `pkb.agents` behind it. This loop writes a research file. | **Amend RT-18 explicitly (RS-86, §5.5)**: the allow-list gains `(research.py, _file_research)` and the loop takes `_write`'s two obligations verbatim — validate before writing, record into `kb_touched`. **The claim that this write "goes through the middleware stack" is withdrawn**: `KbValidationMiddleware` intercepts model tool calls through `wrap_tool_call` and never sees a harness write. What the loop does instead is spelled out as four calls in a fixed order (RS-20). | The corollary is about a model reaching around the permission list, not about harness code writing through it, and LS-1 made exactly this carve-out for the source copy. But RT-18 states that a fourth writer "would need the same argument made again in the same place", and its acceptance test is a grep against the exact three-element set: without the amendment, `make check` fails on a rule this document claims to satisfy. Naming the mechanism is also what makes the rule testable. "Goes through the middleware stack" is not something a test can assert about a call the middleware never sees. |
| **C-40** | **README §1.3 says notes are human-generated and AI-curated**, and this loop produces a synthesis of material the human never wrote. | **The synthesis is not a note.** It is `type.summary` in `sessions/`, which README's own table already puts on the AI-generated, human-curated side, and RS-45 stamps `provenance: researched` so no reader mistakes it for the human's own experience. A conclusion the human wants recorded as their own goes to `notes/` through the ordinary path, in their words. | Filing a synthesis as `type.note` is the trust laundering P-38 names, and it corrupts the one thing the packs load first. The distinction has to be visible in the file rather than remembered by whoever filed it. |
| **C-41** | **RT-39 refuses a turn while an approval is pending**, and a research session spans many turns with a gate at the end. | **The gate is the last step, and the session is `closed` before the approval is raised.** A pending approval blocks the thread, which is correct at that point: the human is deciding whether the file lands. Nothing in the session is waiting on that decision. | An approval raised mid-session would block the checkpoint the session depends on, and the human would have to answer a gate to say "drop the second". P-35 is the same fact from the other side. |
| **C-42** | **VA-32 preserves unknown keys and its hint advises checking for a typo**; this design adds a key that must never be removed. | **Two changes in one commit**: `provenance` joins `KNOWN_FIELDS` (so VA-32 stops firing on it), and the hint gains a clause forbidding removal of a key the model did not add (so every other domain field is safe too). | Adding the field alone fixes this design's case and leaves the trap loaded for `servings:` on a recipe and every other legitimate extension field. The hint is one string and it is read by a model on every successful write. |
| **C-43** | **README §2.2 says the Librarian handles multi-topic work**, and the human's design point 3 asks it to run multi-topic research. This design gives it no research tools and no research file. | **The Librarian fans research out and merges by attribution**, exactly as it fans out a question. Each expert runs its own session inside its own subtree and files its own file. | RS-0d is the human's own ruling, and RT-15 plus "the Librarian writes nothing" leave no legal home for a cross-topic file. Fan-out is not a workaround here: two topics researching one question through their own skills and voices is the same value D11 already established for one source reaching two experts. |
| **C-44** | **Arch §9 promises a free, fast, keyless, networkless test suite**, and this feature's whole point is the internet. | **The vendor sits behind `McpToolset` and the fetch sits behind `pkb.sources`' injectable `Fetcher`**, both already fake-able, and RS-77 makes knowledge-base mode the configuration-free default. The live tier covers vendor behaviour alone (§6.4). **Amended 2026-08-09 (search settled): the seam is `SearchClient` (§5.3a) and the `Fetcher` is off this path.** The resolution holds and its two names changed. `SearchClient` is a two-method Protocol that `fake_search` implements with no socket and no key, so every rule in §1.4 and §1.10 runs against it, and the credential rules of §1.12 run with the variable deliberately unset. `pkb.sources`' injectable `Fetcher` keeps covering the ingestion path (RS-130), where it always belonged. | The Layer 5 build proved this split holds: nothing in its §1 needed a live bot token, because the transport was a Protocol from the first commit. Retrofitting the seam after the tests are written never happens. |
| **C-45** | **TG-77 forbids two channels for one agent in one chat**, and RS-97 opens `Trading · Trend Signal` beside `Trading · Market Regime`. | **The key moves from the agent to the purpose**, and the harm TG-77 names moves with it. §10 carries the requirement. | TG-77's stated harm is an **accidental** split: *"that expert's history splits in half with nothing on screen saying which half a message went to"*. A channel named for its goal says which half on the screen, in the title, so the reason survives the key change intact. |
| **C-46** | **RS-95 refuses a write under `notes/` on any turn that touched external content**, and RS-109 files a lesson there on a `/learn` that may follow a turn with a search in it. | **The refusal binds the model's own write tools; a gated filing is exempt** (RS-95 as amended). | The refusal exists because P-44 leaves the notes path un-gated, and a code-rendered filing the human approved byte for byte is the opposite case: it is gated, its content never passed through the model's hands, and RS-89 re-renders before the write. Refusing it would block the one note in the design that a human explicitly asked for. |
| **C-47** | ~~**README §2.7 describes a session that ends at step 7 with a proposal**~~ **Resolved 2026-08-09.** | **Landed.** README §2.7 opens with *"A channel is a session"*, its step 7 is **Report back** and ends a round rather than a session, and the two directives, the discard outcome and the fan-out are in the human's own words. The sections that moved: §2.7, §1.8 rule 8, §1.3's file-type table and its collaboration rule, §1.4's provenance table, §1.6's *Sessions*, §1.7's session subsection, §1.9's Tier 2, and Part 4. | Kept in the table with its resolution rather than deleted, because a contradiction row that vanishes reads to the next reviewer as one nobody checked. The reason it existed stands: the README is the design in the human's voice, and two documents disagreeing about the lifetime of the central object is the failure this row was opened to stop. |
| **C-49** | **VA-11 is a bijection between `source_type` and the `type.*` tag**, and RS-109's first draft filed a lesson as `source_type: note` tagged `type.solution`. | **The lesson carries `source_type: solution`** (RS-109 as amended). `_SOURCE_TYPES_BY_ROLE[FileRole.NOTE]` is `{note, solution}`, so `notes/` already permits it and no Layer 1 change is needed. | Executed against the built validator: the original pair returns `SOURCE_TYPE_TAG_MISMATCH error VA-11`, and RS-20 calls `validate_content` first, so every lesson would have been refused before reaching the gate. The bijection is derived in code at `validation.py:73` and VA-14's table is derived from it, so there is no second place to fix and no way to carry a `type.*` tag the `source_type` does not name. |
| **C-50** | **README §1.5 says the AI proposes new tags and the human approves them**, and RS-50's tool-keyed gate skips the `NEW_TAG` branch that used to carry that approval. | **RS-124 renders an unregistered tag as its own marked line in the proposal message**, before the human accepts, and the gate's diff shows it again. | Keying the gate by the tool is right for the framing RS-50 argues for, and it costs three branches (RS-50 names all three). Two of them are safe on the diff alone. A new tag is not: it changes the root registry for the whole tree, and a line inside a diff is a thing a human skims. Two chances to see it costs one line of rendering and keeps the tag rule mechanical rather than aspirational. |
| **C-51** | **LB-2 classifies every inbound item on the Librarian's thread**, and RS-121 answers a proposal on that thread without classification. | **Code reads the message before the classifier does**, exactly as RS-100 matches a directive before any model call, and only while a proposal is outstanding. | RS-57 already recorded the failure this prevents in its own words: the human's *"drop the second"* *"would arrive at the Librarian, which classifies it as a fresh inbound item (LB-2) rather than routing it to a session."* LB-2 is a rule about **inbound items**, and an answer to a question the system just asked is not one. The interception is bounded by state rather than by content, so a message arriving with no proposal outstanding takes LB-2's path untouched. |
| **C-52** | **RS-58 puts every external capability in a catalog on `RuntimeConfig` and RS-66 refuses any tool the operator did not allow-list**, and search now arrives through a client no catalog entry describes and no allow-list covers. | **The catalog governs servers, and search is not one.** RS-128 and RS-129 fix the two tool names in code, RS-94 as amended withholds both from every expert model request by rule rather than by a `use` field, and RS-66's allow-list keeps governing every MCP tool unchanged. The configuration surface for search is one credential (RS-133) and one budget (RS-68's `research_search_results`). | RS-66 exists because a gate can only describe a write it understands, so an opaque vendor mutation must be refused at attach. Neither search tool mutates anything: both return text and neither takes a write parameter, which is checkable from their signatures rather than from an operator's assertion. Putting them in the catalog would mean an operator declaring read-only for two functions whose types already say so, and it would let that operator declare the opposite. **The stronger property is what the catalog could not give**: a tool name fixed in code cannot be rebound, so no deployment can hand an expert a search tool on an ordinary turn and route around §1.4. |
| **C-53** | **RS-58 and RG-21 say deployment configuration never comes from knowledge-base content and lives on `RuntimeConfig`**, and RS-133 keeps the search credential off `RuntimeConfig` entirely. | **A credential is not configuration, and the two travel by different routes.** `research_search_results` is configuration and sits on `RuntimeConfig` under RG-21. The key is a secret: `pkb.daemon` reads it, builds the client, and hands the **client** down, so no `RuntimeConfig` field ever holds it as a string. RG-21's rule is untouched, because the thing it governs is which model and which tools an agent gets, and that is still decided by the registry and never by KB content. | TG-24 already ruled this split one layer up and for the same reason: a secret on a config object reaches every `repr`, every debug log and every `/health` body that serialises it, and the Layer 5 spec named a leaked `/health` body as the difference between an embarrassment and a knowledge-base compromise. The repo is public, so the cost of getting this wrong is permanent. Handing down a constructed client rather than a string is what makes the secret unreachable from the layer that would leak it. |
| **C-54** | **RS-52 lets a later session append to an existing research file**, and RS-142 refuses an amendment to a file the amender did not write. | **The origin lives in the file, not in the caller.** A `## Sessions` block naming this thread is what permits the append, so a second enquiry in the same channel appends and a different channel with the same goal is refused. `resolve_note_path(kind="research")` carries both checks (RS-125 as amended). | The append RS-52 was written for is one line of work adding to its own file across weeks, which the origin block permits exactly. The case it was never written for is the one D6 makes unrecoverable: two channels, one slug, and a second session rewriting a first session's evidence with a diff that looks like an append. RS-125 already refuses a taken **lesson** path for the same reason, so this makes the two filing paths agree rather than adding a rule to one of them. |
| **C-55** | **RS-100 makes `/learn` repeatable and RS-102 hands it the whole record**, and §1.13 refuses most of what a long record contains. | **Both hold, and RS-140 is what makes the combination stable.** The distillation reads everything and proposes little; `propose_lessons` returning an empty tuple is the expected result, RS-123 keeps a filed lesson from being proposed twice, and `LEARN_MAX_PROPOSALS` bounds the other end. | Read together without RS-140, the two rules describe a system that asks a model to find something in a nine-month log every time the human types four characters, with five rules telling it what it may not say and nothing telling it that saying nothing is correct. `F-34` shows what the other prior produces, and Hermes affords it with rollback this project does not have. |
| **C-56** | **C-38 rules that no guarantee in this spec is carried by a skill**, and decision BD lets a session write skills. | **Both hold, because the two sentences are about different things.** What a session writes into a skill is **how to work**, and every guarantee in this document stays in code: the gate table, the quote check, the pair selection, the exclusion constant, the two write guards and the origin record. `RS-151` is the test of it, since the exclusions bind a proposed skill from the constant rather than from anything inside the skill's own text. | C-38's reason survives the ruling untouched: a skill is a file the human may edit, so a guarantee living there disappears the day they edit it. The ruling adds a second file the human may edit, which makes C-38's rule more load-bearing rather than less. The failure it prevents is precise and reachable now: a `/learn` that proposed a skill saying *"always check quotes against the fetched bytes"* would read as the admissibility rule and would be the only copy of it a reader ever finds. `RS-17`'s standard settles which side each sentence goes on. |
| **C-57** | **SK-5 says adoption is a permanent fork**, and this project's own `CLAUDE.md` states it as a property of skills in the tree; `RS-143` files skills the human never adopted. | **SK-5 governs a copy that shadows a shipped name, and a new name shadows nothing.** `RS-150` splits the three cases and renders the fork notice for the shipped-name case alone, reusing `adopt_skill`'s own text. A skill named `reverse-sear` forks nothing, because no shipped skill carries that name, so the harness's merge adds it beside the ten rather than over one of them. | The fork is a consequence of **name collision** under `PA-14`'s last-source-wins resolution, and it was stated as a property of adoption because adoption was the only route into the tree. `SK-4` is still the only route for a *copy of a shipped skill*, and `RS-143` opens a second route for a skill that never existed. Reading SK-5 as a property of every skill in the tree would forbid the human's own ruling on a mechanism that does not apply, and it would attach a permanent-fork warning to a file that shadows nothing, which teaches a human to skip the warning that matters. |
| **C-58** | **RT-16 gives the Librarian no write capability in the tree**, and the human placed a process skill in *"librarian root"*. | **The Librarian still writes nothing.** The expert that ran the session calls `file_skill(scope="root")`, the human approves the bytes, and `research.py:_file_skill` performs the write as harness code on RT-18's allow-list, exactly as `create_topic` calls `scaffold_topic` and `adopt_skill` writes `<kb>/skills/<name>/` today (`RS-148`). No `file_skill` tool is bound to the Librarian's graph. | The human's phrase names a **place**, the knowledge-base root, and the design read it as an **actor**. RT-16's own stated reason is about a note needing the topic's skills, voice and `expert.md`, which a process skill does not, so RT-16 does not forbid this on its own terms; `RS-112` and `RS-119` are what forbid it, and both are this document's rules rather than RT-16's. Widening the expert's write permission to reach the root was the alternative and it is refused, because it would loosen RT-15 on every turn to serve one gated filing. |
| **C-59** | **RS-140 says nothing in this design pushes toward producing**, and `RS-143` adds a third thing a `/learn` can produce. | **The prior is unchanged and `RS-151` raises the bar rather than lowering it.** `propose_lessons` returning an empty tuple is still the expected result, `LEARN_EXCLUSIONS` covers all three kinds from one constant, `RS-135` binds hardest on the skill kind, and the prompt test asserts against Hermes's own two biasing sentences whatever the kind. | `F-34` is exactly about a system that learns procedure and is told that producing procedure is the point, so the kind this amendment adds is the kind Hermes's prior was written for. Adding it without restating `RS-140` would import the bias by omission: a model offered a third output and given no counter-prior fills it. `D6` is why the counter-prior costs nothing, since a session that proposes no skill has lost nothing a later `/learn` cannot propose again. |
| **C-48** | **C-40 says the synthesis is not a note**, and RS-109 files a lesson as `type.solution` under `notes/`. | **Both hold, and the distinction is the provenance value.** A synthesis of things read stays `type.summary` in `sessions/` with `provenance: researched`; earned experience files as `type.solution` with `provenance: practised`. | C-40's danger is filing *researched* material as the human's own experience, which is the laundering P-36 names. A lesson from trials the human ran is their own experience, and the field says which route it took. Collapsing the two would make `provenance` decorative on the one file class it was added for. |

---

## 4. Open questions for the human

Ranked by blast radius. **Every one has a default already encoded in §1**, so implementation is not
blocked.

| # | Question | Options | Recommended default | Blast radius if changed later |
|---|----------|---------|---------------------|-------------------------------|
| **Q37** | **How does the MCP client get built?** | (a) raw `mcp` client behind our own `McpToolset` Protocol, adapting each tool to a `StructuredTool`; (b) `langchain-mcp-adapters` behind the same Protocol; (c) both, chosen per server. | **(b), subject to one measurement before the commit.** Executed here: `mcp` is importable and `langchain_mcp_adapters` is not, so (b) adds a package. Q18's aiogram measurement is the test to repeat: check the dependency closure, and refuse it if it pulls a second HTTP stack. If the closure is `mcp` plus `langchain-core`, both already installed, take it, because unlike PTB there is no 95% of the library our shape forbids using. (a) is roughly 60 lines and stays the fallback. **Amended 2026-08-09 (search settled): this question no longer blocks search, and its own measurement now has a worked example.** Original recommendation, kept and unchanged. Q38 used to ride on this row, so an unanswered MCP dependency question held up the internet pillar; `P-50` cut that link and §8 moves the search work into increment 4 ahead of MCP entirely. The measurement this row demands was run for `ollama` and is written down in `P-50`: the closure adds `httpx` and `pydantic`, both declared, and pulls no second HTTP stack. Running the same check for `langchain-mcp-adapters` before its commit is the outstanding half. | One module. The Protocol is the whole point: every rule in §1.8 is written against `McpToolset` and none of them changes with the answer. **And search is no longer among them**, so a slow answer here costs a recipe server rather than the research loop. |
| **Q38** | ~~**Where does web search come from?**~~ **Resolved 2026-08-09 by execution.** | (a) an MCP server like any other (decision AR); (b) a vendor SDK in `pkb.agents`; (c) a built-in fetch-and-scrape with no search engine; **(d) the model provider's own client, which ships search and is already installed**. | **(d), and no vendor comparison was needed to reach it.** The row is kept with its resolution rather than deleted, for C-47's reason: a question that vanishes reads to the next reader as one nobody answered. `P-50` settled it: `ollama.web_search` and `ollama.web_fetch` sit in the venv today, arrive through `langchain-ollama`, and pull only `httpx` and `pydantic`, both declared. **(a) was the recommended default and it was wrong for a reason worth keeping**: it optimised for one mechanism across every external capability, which is right when the capabilities are strangers to each other and wrong when one of them is the provider already running the models. (b) is the shape that landed, with the objection answered rather than accepted: the key stays out of `RuntimeConfig` (RS-133, C-53) and the import contracts need no exception, because `pkb.agents` already imports this client's transport for its models. (c) stays refused for the reason given. **One consequence is unverified and stated rather than guessed**: `F-30` records that whether search is included in the Ollama Pro plan or metered separately could not be determined, and the docs path 404s. RS-68's `research_search_results` bounds the calls either way. | ~~Changes `McpServerSpec` into a union~~ **Nothing, now that it is settled.** RS-128 and RS-129 are two functions behind two tool names; swapping the provider later changes that module and no rule in §1.4, because RS-127 is a property of the fetch log rather than of the searcher. |
| **Q39** | **Does the human get to see and edit the plan before researchers run?** | (a) no, the first checkpoint is after round 1; (b) yes, a plan checkpoint before any researcher runs; (c) configurable. | **(a) for v1** (RS-12). Round 1 is bounded by RS-68 and costs three researchers at 12 steps; showing the plan first doubles the turns for a session whose first round is cheap and whose results are the thing the human can actually judge. (b) is the right later addition and needs no new rule beyond one more `awaiting-direction` entry point. State the cost plainly: a badly aimed round 1 is wasted, and the human sees the misaim only after it ran. | One extra state transition and one checkpoint renderer. Fully reversible, and (b) becomes attractive the moment a bound server costs real money per call. |
| **Q40** | **Does a research session ever run unattended?** | (a) never, a checkpoint always waits for a human; (b) a configurable auto-continue for N rounds; (c) auto-continue until a budget stops it. | **(a)** (RS-12). The feature the human asked for is walking through research together, and (c) is a machine writing the human's prior with nobody watching.
